16:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
17:T4bb,BitLM represents each tokenizer entry with a fixed-length binary code and attaches a lightweight diffusion head on top of a standard Transformer backbone.
- Representation: each token maps to a B-bit code (fixed length). Codes are projected into the model hidden space as the discrete target representation.
- Division of labor: the causal Transformer backbone computes contextual latents ("what should happen next"), while the diffusion head performs iterative denoising in bit space to realize the continuation as discrete symbols ("how it crystallizes into tokens").
- Block-causal generation: by changing the attention mask from fully causal to block-causal, BitLM preserves strict left-to-right dependencies across blocks while allowing parallel iterative refinement of multiple future positions within a block. Parallelism is controlled by block size.
- Key differences: unlike speculative/blockwise decoding, BitLM changes the output interface rather than accelerating softmax sampling; unlike fully non-causal diffusion LMs, it keeps a causal backbone and block-level causality; unlike AR multi-token heads, jointness comes from coupled bit denoising rather than independent per-position classification.18:T868,方法由两个模块组成。第一阶段是非循环 backbone，文中实现为 Transformer，它根据上下文先产生每个位置的初始输出 embedding，相当于对下一个 token 的隐表示做一次快速猜测。第二阶段是 attractor module，一个通常更小的循环网络，它不直接输出 token，而是接收 backbone 的初始 embedding，并通过迭代映射把该 embedding 推向一个固定点。最终在这个平衡态 embedding 上做解码，得到词表分布。

关键改动在于训练方式。传统 looped LM 会把 recurrent block 展开 T 步，然后做 BPTT，训练内存和计算都随 T 增长。Attractor Model 则把 refinement 写成 z*=f(z*; x, h0) 这样的 fixed-point equation，其中 h0 是 backbone 的初始提议。前向时用求解器迭代到收敛，反向时不对所有中间步做显式回传，而是用 implicit differentiation 计算梯度，因此训练显存不再线性依赖迭代步数。这一点直接借鉴 DEQ，但论文的设计不是把整层网络都做成 equilibrium，而是只在输出 embedding 空间上求平衡，降低了优化难度，也保留了标准 Transformer backbone 的表达和并行优势。

作者强调两阶段结构的重要性：backbone 负责给出一个足够接近解的初值，attractor 只做局部修正。这样做比“从零开始循环推理”更稳定，也更便宜。另一个重要点是 adaptive refinement。因为前向是按收敛停止，训练和推理时每个 token 所需的迭代次数可以不同，不再受固定 loop depth 限制。这比显式 looped Transformer 更自然地支持 test-time compute 调节。

论文还提出 equilibrium internalization：随着训练进行，backbone 输出会越来越接近 attractor 的固定点，导致求解器需要的步数逐渐减少。这个现象不是额外蒸馏损失带来的，而是在纯 next-token 训练下自发出现。可以把它理解为：固定点求解器在训练早期像一个老师，提供更好的隐表示目标；训练后期 backbone 学会直接落在这个 attractor basin 附近，于是慢路径被部分吸收到快路径里。19:T483,This is a meaningful contribution for pretraining and model architecture research. First, it moves recurrent latent computation from an interesting but fragile idea toward a more usable architectural primitive: effective refinement depth no longer implies linearly growing training memory, and the number of refinement steps is chosen adaptively by convergence rather than fixed in advance. Second, it brings DEQ-style implicit layers back into language modeling in a more practical form. Instead of making the whole Transformer an equilibrium system, it uses a standard backbone plus a smaller attractor module that solves for equilibrium only in output-embedding space. That is a much more realistic design for large-scale LMs. Third, the observed phenomenon of equilibrium internalization is important. Even though the model is trained only with next-token loss, the backbone gradually learns to produce embeddings closer to the fixed point, reducing the need for iterative solving. That has direct implications for latent reasoning, train-time versus test-time compute, and how slow internal computation may be absorbed into a fast path over training.1a:T841,The architecture has two stages. A non-recurrent backbone, implemented as a Transformer, first proposes an initial output embedding for each token position. This is a fast latent guess for the next-token representation. A separate attractor module, typically smaller and recurrent, then refines that embedding by iterating toward a fixed point. The final token distribution is decoded from the equilibrium embedding rather than from the backbone proposal alone.

The key change is in how the recurrent part is trained. Standard looped language models explicitly unroll the recurrent block for T steps and backpropagate through all steps, which makes memory and optimization cost scale with T. Attractor Models instead formulate refinement as a fixed-point equation, roughly of the form z* = f(z*; x, h0), where h0 is the backbone proposal. The forward pass runs a solver until convergence, and the backward pass uses implicit differentiation rather than storing and differentiating through every intermediate iterate. This is the main source of constant-memory behavior with respect to refinement depth.

A second important design choice is the two-stage decomposition. The backbone is responsible for producing a good initialization, while the attractor performs local correction. This is more stable and cheaper than asking a recurrent module to do all the work from scratch. The model also supports adaptive refinement naturally: different tokens can require different numbers of solver iterations at train and test time, depending on convergence. That is a cleaner route to variable test-time compute than fixed-depth looped Transformers.

The paper also identifies equilibrium internalization. During training, the backbone’s initial prediction moves closer to the attractor’s fixed point, so the solver needs fewer iterations to reach approximate equilibrium. This happens without any explicit distillation loss. One useful interpretation is that the equilibrium solver acts like a latent teacher early in training, and the backbone gradually absorbs part of that computation into its direct prediction.1b:T759,The experiments cover two regimes: large-scale language-model pretraining and hard reasoning with tiny models. In language modeling, Attractor Models are compared against standard feed-forward Transformers and explicitly unrolled looped baselines. The reported result is that Attractor Models achieve better language-modeling performance under comparable training budgets while remaining more stable and avoiding the linear memory growth associated with increasing recurrent depth in looped baselines. The constant-memory claim with respect to effective refinement depth is one of the central empirical results.

In the reasoning regime, the model outperforms prior tiny recursive reasoners on difficult tasks and appears to mitigate the pathological scaling behavior sometimes seen in small recurrent models, where increasing model size can hurt performance. The paper attributes this to the stability and inductive bias of fixed-point refinement compared with explicit recurrent unrolling.

The most interesting evidence is in the training dynamics rather than a single benchmark score. The paper shows convergence behavior and accuracy-versus-iteration curves indicating that, as training progresses, the backbone proposal gets closer to the final fixed point and the number of solver iterations needed for approximate convergence decreases. Performance does not collapse as this happens. That is the clearest empirical signature of equilibrium internalization.

When reading the full paper, the most important numbers to extract are: the relative size of the attractor versus the backbone, average solver iterations and how they change over training, memory and compute differences versus looped baselines, and the exact gains on language modeling and reasoning benchmarks. Those details determine whether the method is only elegant conceptually or also compelling in practice.1c:T537,This one deserves a careful read. On the first pass, focus on the problem framing in the introduction and the main architecture figure. The key idea to understand is why “backbone proposal plus attractor refinement” should be more stable than a fully recurrent language model. On the second pass, read the fixed-point formulation, solver details, and implicit differentiation carefully. That is where the practical value of the method lives.

For the experiments, read along three tracks. First, inspect the main language-modeling comparisons and check whether the training budgets and baselines are aligned fairly. Second, spend time on the memory, compute, and convergence analyses. Those matter more than raw benchmark scores because they answer whether recurrent depth can be used at scale. Third, study the figures related to equilibrium internalization: solver steps over training, distance between the backbone proposal and the fixed point, and accuracy as a function of refinement iterations. Those plots contain the most interesting research signal.

If you work on latent reasoning, DEQ-style models, recurrent Transformers, or test-time compute, this is easily a 60-90 minute read. Start with the method and training-dynamics figures, then verify the solver settings, stabilization tricks, and ablations in the appendix.1d:T6cd,The work has three layers. First is the theoretical analysis. Starting from a standard sparse top-k MoE layer, the authors derive that for a token routed to expert i, the router weight vector associated with expert i and the expert weights processing that token receive gradients along the same input hidden-state direction, differing mainly by scalar coefficients. This is stronger than the generic statement that both modules are in the same computation graph. It means matched router–expert pairs accumulate updates from the same routed-token history and therefore evolve as coupled geometric directions. Second is empirical validation. In a 1B-scale SMoE trained from scratch, the paper compares router scores with the internal activations of experts on the same token and finds that experts ranked higher by the router also respond more strongly internally. So routing is not merely an external assignment layer; it is mirrored in expert computation. Third is a reinterpretation of the auxiliary load-balancing loss. Because this loss sends gradients to every router direction on every token, not only to selected experts, it mixes expert-specific directions and weakens the natural coupling induced by routed-token history. The paper supports this both theoretically and empirically. Motivated by that view, the authors introduce a parameter-free centroid router: each expert maintains an online K-Means or EMA centroid of the hidden states assigned to it, and routing is done by cosine similarity to these centroids, with sign-updated biases for load control. The point is not to beat learned routers outright, but to test whether a large part of routing can be explained as geometric summarization of expert-specific token regions.1e:T4b1,The experiments address three questions. First, does geometric coupling appear in practice? On a roughly 1B-parameter SMoE trained from scratch on about 50B tokens, the paper shows that experts receiving higher router scores also exhibit stronger internal activations for the same token, which supports the coupling hypothesis. Second, how does auxiliary balancing alter geometry? The authors train two otherwise matched 1B SMoEs differing only in the balancing rule and measure similarity among router directions. With the auxiliary loss, distinct router vectors become nearly three times more similar, indicating stronger directional homogenization. Load balance improves, but specialization weakens. Third, can a centroid router recover much of the learned router’s function? The answer is largely yes: the centroid router achieves the lowest load imbalance among the tested variants while keeping perplexity close to the learned router trained without the auxiliary loss, with only a modest degradation. The paper is careful not to conflate better utilization with better language modeling; the empirical emphasis is on explaining routing behavior rather than claiming absolute perplexity gains.1f:T46d,This one deserves a careful read. Start with the theory section and work through the gradient derivation; the key idea is why router and expert updates align along the same token direction. Without that, the later geometric claims can sound more intuitive than they really are. Then read the plots comparing router scores and expert activations, since they turn the theory into an observable training phenomenon. After that, focus on the analysis of the auxiliary load-balancing loss and the router-direction similarity statistics; this is the part with the clearest practical consequence for MoE training. Finish with the centroid-router section, especially the online K-Means or EMA update rule, cosine routing, and bias adjustment. The paper is best read as a mechanism paper, not as a benchmark paper. Two follow-up questions are especially worth thinking about: whether the same coupling survives in industrial settings with top-2 routing, shared experts, and capacity constraints; and whether one can design balancing signals that regulate load without injecting strongly direction-collapsing gradients into all router vectors.20:T501,Three core innovations differentiate DisagMoE from prior work: 1. Disaggregated component placement: Attention layers and MoE FFN layers are assigned to separate worker groups. Attention groups replicate dense components and split data by micro-batch, while FFN groups shard experts and process tokens according to routing results. Each group can host multiple layers of the same component type, maximizing device utilization under memory constraints and eliminating the OOM issue of prior disaggregation schemes that required each group to store all layers of their assigned component. 2. AF-Pipe multi-stage pipeline: Inter-group many-to-many token exchange is treated as an independent pipeline stage, aligned with attention and FFN computation stage boundaries to enable systematic overlap of communication with both types of computation, replacing the on-critical-path all-to-all communication in conventional EP. 3. Adaptive resource allocation: A lightweight compute-communication roofline model guides dynamic adjustment of attention/FFN group sizes and NIC bandwidth allocation between groups, adapting to imbalance across different sequence lengths, top-k routing policies, and EP scales to expand the effective overlap window and reduce critical-path communication time.21:T582,First, sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are used to decompose residual stream activations of 4 DLMs (MDLM, SEDD, DREAM, LLaDA) with different architectures, training objectives, and parameter scales ranging from 124M to 8B (60x scale range) into sparse interpretable features. Three core properties of semantic attribute formation are summarized: 1) Different attributes have distinct commitment timings: topic is committed within the first 2% of denoising steps, while sentiment forms gradually over 20% of the process; 2) Commitment sharpness varies: some attributes form in a narrow time window, while others form uniformly across the entire denoising trajectory; 3) Effect-size asymmetry: the per-feature discriminative strength varies across attributes. Based on these properties, an adaptive intervention scheduling strategy is derived: intervention is concentrated in the time window when the target attribute is actively forming, with no disturbance applied in other steps. This is formalized as a budget-constrained optimization problem, where the optimal schedule is proportional to the gain-cost ratio of each step. For multi-attribute control, intervention is implemented via disjoint SAE feature sets, cross-attribute interference is bounded by the decoder Gram structure, and effect-size calibration is applied to balance the strength of different attributes to avoid weak attributes being overwhelmed.22:T9ae,MuonQ has three main components, all built around the idea of preventing quantization noise from inducing systematic directional drift.

The first is pre-quantization normalization. The authors argue that if gradients or momentum are quantized directly at each step, the absolute scale of the quantization error changes with the state magnitude. Over many steps, this can accumulate into a preferred direction. Since Muon preserves direction and discards singular-value magnitudes, such bias is especially damaging. MuonQ normalizes the state before quantization so that each step contributes errors of comparable magnitude. The goal is not simply to reduce one-step MSE, but to make accumulated error more isotropic over time.

The second is structural decomposition. Direct low-bit quantization of the full momentum matrix can perturb singular vectors, and those perturbations survive the orthogonalization step. MuonQ instead decomposes the momentum structurally and quantizes the singular factors separately, so that quantization error is pushed more into singular-value magnitudes than into singular-vector directions. This matches Muon’s update rule: singular-value errors are largely washed out by the polar operation, while directional errors are preserved. The paper also proposes a truncated top-k variant to trade off memory and fidelity. The important point is not decomposition for its own sake, but deliberate error reallocation into the part of the representation that Muon is less sensitive to.

The third is μ-law companding quantization. The authors argue that Muon needs higher resolution near zero because, after polar decomposition, different singular directions are treated more evenly than in Adam-like optimizers. Uniform quantization wastes bins on large magnitudes and under-resolves the dense region near zero, where subtle directional information can still matter. Companding compresses the dynamic range before quantization and allocates more effective resolution to that dense region.

Together, these components form a coherent design: normalization controls the geometry of accumulated error, structural decomposition controls where the error lands, and companding improves resolution where Muon is most sensitive. Compared with prior Muon quantization work, the novelty is that the method is derived from the optimizer’s polar-update mechanism rather than from generic low-bit heuristics or mixed-precision retention of dominant components.23:T796,The main empirical claim is focused and important: on GPT-style and LLaMA-style pretraining setups, pure 4-bit MuonQ tracks full-precision Muon closely in both training loss and downstream accuracy, while reducing optimizer-state memory by up to 7.3x. This matters because the paper positions prior work as follows: 8-bit Muon is generally workable, but naive pure 4-bit quantization degrades badly, and existing methods often rely on mixed precision for dominant singular components. MuonQ is meant to close that gap.

The most informative experiments are likely four types. First, training-curve comparisons among full-precision Muon, naive 4-bit Muon, prior 8-bit or mixed-precision baselines, and MuonQ. The shape of the loss curve matters as much as the endpoint. Second, downstream evaluations after pretraining, to verify that low-bit optimizer states do not silently damage generalization. Third, ablations over the three components: normalization only, decomposition only, companding only, and different top-k truncation settings. If the paper is convincing, these should show complementary gains rather than a single dominant trick. Fourth, mechanism-oriented analyses such as singular-vector perturbation, directional similarity after quantization, or deviation in the polar update. Those are the experiments that directly support the directional-fidelity thesis.

The hard facts visible from the provided text are: experiments cover GPT-style and LLaMA-style models; the target is pure 4-bit Muon state quantization; MuonQ remains close to full-precision Muon in training loss and downstream accuracy; and optimizer-state memory is reduced by up to 7.3x. When reading the full paper, it is worth extracting the exact model sizes, token budgets, quantization granularity, top-k settings, and the absolute gaps to Muon and Adam baselines, because those details determine whether the method is likely to transfer to large-scale runs.24:T712,This one deserves a careful read. There are also several follow-up questions worth keeping in mind. Can the same principle extend to other matrix-structured optimizers, or to structured-state methods such as Shampoo or Adafactor? Can directional fidelity be turned into a more explicit objective or error bound rather than a design principle? That last point matters for the real system-level payoff.25:T746,Hebatron is built on NVIDIA Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B-Base-BF16, a sparse MoE model with roughly 30B total parameters and about 3B active parameters per forward pass. The base model choice serves two purposes. First, sparse activation keeps localization and inference efficient. Second, Nemotron’s documented pretraining distribution allows the authors to reintroduce selected original data as an anti-forgetting anchor during continued pretraining.

The training recipe uses a three-phase easy-to-hard curriculum. From the introduction, the intended progression is clear even if the exact mixture details are not fully visible in the excerpt: start with more regular, formal, and morphologically stable Hebrew sources such as literary or legal text, then move toward broader and noisier web-style data that demands more world knowledge and reasoning. The paper explicitly compares this ordering against the reversed curriculum, which is important because it turns curriculum from a narrative choice into an experimentally tested variable.

A second key component is continuous anti-forgetting anchoring. Instead of localizing on Hebrew alone and trying to recover English later, the model is co-trained throughout continued pretraining with a curated subset of the foundation model’s original signal, especially high-quality English reasoning data. The goal is to retain baseline reasoning and English proficiency while improving Hebrew. After continued pretraining, the model is supervised fine-tuned on 2 million bilingual Hebrew-English samples, which likely serves both instruction adaptation and cross-lingual alignment.

There is no new MoE routing method or tokenizer innovation here. The contribution is the training recipe: sparse MoE as the base, staged curriculum for localization, continuous anchoring against forgetting, and bilingual SFT on top.26:T5d0,The headline findings are straightforward. First, curriculum order matters: using the intended easy-to-hard curriculum yields about a 3-point aggregate benchmark gain over the reversed ordering. That is probably the most informative result from a pretraining perspective, because it isolates data sequencing as a meaningful factor in continued pretraining. Second, the final model reaches a Hebrew reasoning average of 73.8%, outperforming the open-source Hebrew baselines considered by the authors and remaining competitive with Gemma-3-27B-IT on tasks such as GSM8K-HE and Israeli Trivia. Third, because the model is a 30B-A3B sparse MoE, only around 3B parameters are active per forward pass, and the paper reports roughly 9x higher inference throughput at native context length.

The more interesting interpretation is in the trade-off. The model keeps a relatively large total parameter budget for knowledge capacity, but uses sparse activation to control cost. Based on the visible content, the strongest evidence is the 3-point curriculum effect plus the combination of strong Hebrew performance and efficient sparse inference.27:T412,This is a skim paper. If reading more deeply, four questions matter most: whether the original tokenizer is reused and how much that hurts Hebrew morphology, how the anchor ratio is chosen, whether MoE experts show language-specific routing bias, and whether the curriculum gain comes from true difficulty ordering or simply from presenting cleaner data earlier.28:T64d,The method has two main pieces: a lightweight patch selector called ReVision Token Selection (RTS), and end-to-end fine-tuning of an MLLM on filtered trajectories. RTS is a 3-layer MLP that takes the embeddings of corresponding patches from two consecutive screenshots and predicts whether the current patch is redundant given the previous frame. The design choice here is important: the model does not perform global image compression or frame dropping. Instead, it keeps the original patch grid and removes only redundant patches at aligned spatial locations, which preserves compatibility with an existing backbone such as Qwen2.5-VL-7B.

After training RTS, the selector is inserted into the data pipeline. Historical screenshots in AgentNet trajectories are filtered patch-wise, and the MLLM is fine-tuned directly on these compact visual histories. This is a stronger setup than post-hoc token pruning at inference time, because the model is exposed during training to temporally sparse visual evidence and can learn to integrate information distributed across multiple steps.

Relative to common baselines, the contribution is fairly clean. It does not simply reduce the number of history images; it reduces redundancy within each image so that longer temporal context fits under the same budget. It does not rely on hand-crafted pixel differencing; redundancy is predicted in embedding space, which is closer to the model’s own representation. And it does not modify the backbone architecture, so the gains can be attributed mainly to temporal token filtering plus training on the filtered distribution.29:T61b,Experiments are run on OSWorld, WebTailBench, and AgentNetBench with Qwen2.5-VL-7B as the base model. The motivating analysis is already useful on its own: consecutive GUI screenshots contain 45.4% unchanged patches on average, and in some settings the redundancy exceeds 56%, corresponding to more than 1,000 redundant patches per step.

On the main benchmarks, using 5 history screenshots, ReVision reduces visual token usage by about 46% on average while improving success rate by roughly 3 percentage points over the no-drop baseline. With only 3 history images, it reaches performance close to some stronger baselines while using about half as many visual tokens. The more interesting result is the trend with history length: once the history grows to 5 images or more, ReVision consistently outperforms most same-scale baselines by at least around 2 points on average. The key takeaway is not a single headline number but the shift in the history-performance curve. Without redundancy reduction, performance appears to saturate early; with filtered histories, adding more past context continues to help. That supports the claim that prior saturation was driven by inefficient visual representations rather than by the irrelevance of history itself.

The ablations appear to reinforce this story: learned patch selection beats naive history truncation, training on filtered trajectories is better than applying filtering only at inference, and keeping more time steps with sparse patches is better than keeping fewer full frames under a similar token budget.2a:T428,This is a skim paper, but a careful skim is worthwhile. If reading more deeply, three questions are worth checking: how the RTS supervision is constructed, whether semantically important but visually stable regions can be dropped by mistake, and whether the same temporal filtering idea could be extended from GUI trajectories to native video pretraining.2b:T442,SOMA (Soft-prompts for lOcal Manifold Approximation) is a three-stage per-session adaptation pipeline.
1) Soft-prompt tuning: using early turns, learn soft prompts for the small model that intentionally expose directions where the small and large models diverge most in semantic behavior (maximizing behavioral/semantic divergence). The idea is to find the most diagnostic local directions before aligning.
2) Localized fine-tuning: collect a small set of input–output pairs from the large model in the early session and perform localized adaptation of the small model (via LoRA) to match the large model within this session-specific region. The key design choice is local, session-scoped fitting instead of global distillation.
3) Efficient inference: run the adapted small model in a prompt-free manner, feed it an extractive summary to reduce input length, and use a cosine-similarity gate to decide when to switch/rollback to the large model upon topic shift.
Overall, the method reframes multi-turn serving as session-level local imitation rather than cache reuse or generic routing.2c:T45a,Ada-MK adapts MegaKernel to Ada GPUs by pushing decisions offline and minimizing runtime control overhead:
1) Adaptive shared-memory management: model shared-memory usage as a function of hardware, model architecture, and workload; reduce peak shared-memory demand (claimed ~50%) via fine-grained splitting along the K dimension, and enable cross-operator page reuse to fit viable pipelines within Ada’s 128KB/SM constraint.
2) Fine-grained DAG-based auto-search: build PTX-level dependency DAGs via MLIR lowering, then profile offline to select and serialize an optimal execution trace. At runtime, execute the fixed trace to avoid the if-else branching and dynamic scheduling overhead seen in approaches like managed-pointer mechanisms.
3) Heterogeneous hybrid engine: integrate as a TensorRT-LLM plugin—use TensorRT-LLM native operators for Prefill (better generality for long sequences/large batches) and switch to MegaKernel for Decode (to cut launches and HBM traffic). The key design choice is explicitly separating prefill vs decode implementations instead of forcing one kernel strategy to cover both.2d:T8bd,The method partitions a sequence into non-overlapping segments and decomposes cross-segment information flow into two channels. The first is a differentiable local continuity channel, C_i, implemented as a fixed-size carried KV tail. This is the only recurrent interface state passed from one segment to the next, and it is used identically in training and inference. The second is a forward-only long-range conditioning channel, R_i, implemented as a retrieved KV prefix. It gives access to evidence beyond the carried-KV horizon, but it does not participate in state recursion and gradients do not flow backward through it.

Under this design, each segment uses the same forward operator at training and inference: outputs for the current segment depend on current tokens, the carried state C_{i-1}, and the retrieved prefix R_{i-1}. The crucial constraint is that only C defines the recursive state chain. R is auxiliary conditioning, not part of the recurrent state. Training uses TBPTT, but not as an approximation to full-attention learning. Instead, the objective itself restricts cross-segment credit assignment to the most recent K state transitions. Because the model is explicitly defined under this bounded recursion, TBPTT computes the exact gradient of the inference-consistent objective. The paper further claims that K=1 is sufficient and optimal in practice, which is a strong statement about how short the necessary differentiable horizon can be.

Architecturally, the design is realized with head- and layer-sparse long-range heads. A small subset of heads handles long-range retrieval or conditioning, while most heads support local state-carrying computation. The motivation is straightforward: if all heads are responsible for long-range dependency handling, the recurrent interface becomes too large and the execution semantics become harder to control. By confining long-range access to sparse substructures, the model keeps a clean boundary between local recurrent state and non-recurrent long-range evidence. The trade-off is explicit: the method does not try to preserve exact full-attention semantics. It defines a more restricted but deployment-aligned execution model and optimizes directly for that regime.2e:T4fa,From the provided full text, three main empirical claims are clear. First, the framework is validated on multiple long-context benchmarks and context lengths, showing strong performance under constrained execution. That suggests the consistency constraint does not collapse long-range capability even though state propagation is heavily restricted. Second, ablations on the truncation horizon show that TBPTT with K=1 is already sufficient and optimal. This is an important result because it implies that the useful differentiable cross-segment learning signal can be extremely short, which reduces both training cost and implementation complexity. Third, at 128K-context prefill, the method uses about 6× less memory than full attention. So the proposal is not only conceptually cleaner; it also yields substantial systems-level savings.

The details that matter most when reading the full paper are the segment length, how the retrieved KV prefix is constructed, how many heads are allocated to long-range access, and how the method compares against the common baseline of full-attention training with segmented inference. It is also important to check whether the gains show up only in average benchmark scores or also in inference stability under constrained execution.2f:T458,Start with the method overview and the main figure that defines the two channels, C and R. The easiest mistake is to treat R as part of the recurrent memory. It is not. R is forward-only conditioning, while C is the only true recurrent state. Then read the objective formalization carefully. The most informative part of the paper is the argument for why TBPTT is exact in this setting rather than an approximation. After that, inspect the architectural realization with sparse long-range heads, because that determines whether the proposal is mainly a conceptual framework or a practical Transformer variant. Finish with the ablations: K=1, segment size, the necessity of retrieval prefixes, and the effect of head/layer sparsity.

If you only want a quick triage, 30 minutes is enough for the introduction, the method figure, the objective definition, the K ablation, and the 128K memory comparison. If you are considering this for pretraining or streaming LM work, the appendix is likely important, especially the exact interface-state size, retrieval-prefix construction, and throughput or stability details.30:T480,KV-Fold splits a long sequence into chunks. At chunk t, the model attends to the accumulated KV cache from all previous chunks as a prefix, computes the current chunk, appends the newly produced keys and values, and forwards the enlarged cache to chunk t+1. The authors frame this as a left fold where the KV cache is the accumulator and the same one-step update is applied repeatedly. Three aspects matter. First, the method is training-free: no finetuning, no memory tokens, no modified attention pattern, no learned compression. Second, past information remains uncompressed in the cache, so long-range retrieval still relies on content-based addressing rather than summary vectors. Third, the method occupies a different point from streaming approaches: it accepts linearly growing cache in exchange for preserving access to the full history. The paper also clarifies why outputs differ from a monolithic full-context forward pass. Chunk boundaries alter the organization of attention computation, so exact equivalence is not expected; empirically, however, the discrepancy behaves like a stable regime shift rather than cumulative numerical drift.31:T4a6,The evaluation focuses on stability, retrieval, and resource trade-offs. On stability, per-step drift rises during the first few chunk transitions and then plateaus, remaining nearly constant even for deep chains. The paper stresses that this plateau is robust to chunk size, model family, and even a 10,000x change in numerical precision. On retrieval, the needle-in-a-haystack benchmark spans 16K, 32K, 64K, 96K, and 128K contexts. On Llama-3.1-8B with its native 128K window, KV-Fold recovers 100% of inserted facts at all tested distances, including chain depth 511. On resources, the comparison is concrete: at T=128K, a single full-attention forward is infeasible on a 40GB A100 because the attention score matrix alone is around 1TB, whereas KV-Fold fits with about 35.6GB peak memory and finishes in 171 seconds. Against StreamingLLM, KV-Fold uses more memory because its cache grows with sequence length; the paper reports roughly 16.6GB peak for a 1024-token StreamingLLM cache versus about 17.18GB cache growth for KV-Fold in a comparable setting, with higher total working memory. The trade is retrieval quality: KV-Fold keeps 100% recall where streaming degrades with distance.32:T775,The method starts from the standard layer reconstruction objective and rewrites it as minimizing a Hessian-weighted distance between pretrained weights and quantized weights under discrete quantization constraints. It then applies ADMM with variable splitting: one variable is optimized in continuous space to reduce reconstruction error, while another is projected onto the quantization set, and the augmented Lagrangian gradually enforces consistency between them. Unlike GPTQ, which compensates errors sequentially after each column is quantized, ADMM-Q updates the whole layer jointly, so it is not tied to a processing order.

Three implementation details are central. First, diagonal scaling is used to improve conditioning. The authors point out that LLM activations exhibit strong channel outliers, making X^T X ill-conditioned and direct ADMM unstable. They therefore rescale channels before optimization, effectively preconditioning the Hessian. This is conceptually related to outlier-handling methods such as SmoothQuant, but the purpose here is solver stability rather than distribution reshaping for quantization itself. Second, they use an adaptive penalty schedule. If the ADMM penalty is too large too early, optimization collapses prematurely onto the discrete set; if too small, convergence to feasible quantized weights is slow. The schedule lets the method first behave more like a Hessian-aware continuous fit and only later tighten the quantization constraint. Third, they add a local-search refinement step after ADMM converges to a discrete solution, correcting suboptimal grid assignments left by the approximate nonconvex optimization.

The paper also extends prior ADMM convergence analysis from pruning constraints to quantization constraints. This should be read as a stability result for the iterative procedure, not as a guarantee of global optimality for the discrete problem.33:T54c,From the full text provided, the experimental story is clear even though not all tables are visible. The paper evaluates both weight-only and weight-activation settings, mainly on the Qwen3 family. ADMM-Q consistently improves over GPTQ in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, with the largest gains in sub-4-bit regimes such as W3. It also plugs into existing pipelines such as SpinQuant and SmoothQuant and still improves results, which suggests the benefit is not tied to a specific preprocessing trick. On the serving side, deployment in vLLM preserves the same throughput and memory advantages as GPTQ, so the quality gains do not appear to come at inference-time cost.

The most informative experiments are likely threefold. First, the main comparisons at 3-bit and 4-bit, where solver quality matters most. Second, ablations removing diagonal scaling, adaptive penalty scheduling, and local search, to separate numerical conditioning effects from optimization-path effects and discrete refinement. Third, compatibility tests across model sizes and quantization pipelines, which matter if the method is to be treated as a drop-in replacement.

The visible hard number is that in vLLM, Qwen-32B at W8A8 retains about 1.5x inference speedup while using ADMM-Q. The paper’s gains are reported in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy rather than throughput.34:T491,This is a skim paper rather than a line-by-line derivation paper. Start with the introduction to frame it correctly: the contribution is not a new quantization scheme, but a new solver for an existing Hessian-aware objective. Then read the method section with attention to four pieces: the Hessian-weighted formulation, diagonal scaling, adaptive penalty scheduling, and local-search refinement. Those are the real differences from GPTQ. After that, jump to the main result tables and focus on W3 and W4. If the gains are not robust there, the method is much less interesting.
